[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 16.8 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '# Codex self-knowledge\r\n\r\nUse this manual-first route only for genuinely broad Codex setup, orientation, customization, troubleshooting, local-state guidance, or system-map synthesis across skills, plugins, MCP, hooks, `AGENTS.md`, automations, and product surfaces. Mixed Chat/Work/Codex comparisons belong to `official-docs.md` instead.\r\n\r\nNarrow Codex documentation questions require official documentation search first, then an actual page open or fetch using an available documentation or official-domain web capability. This includes a single feature such as Codex Goals, a specific setting, documented behavior, exact error, or requested page citation. Search and fetch the exact official topic before inspecting local files or bundled references. Do not fetch the manual, read bundled references, inspect local configuration or caches, or turn a targeted documentation lookup into broad product synthesis. Current or latest model questions follow the model-selection route.\r\n\r\n## Start with the manual\r\n\r\nReuse a manual path and outline path already established in the same thread when both remain usable and current. Refresh before relying on a path fetched more than about a day ago, obtained from another thread or uncertain source, or missing likely-current information.\r\n\r\nOtherwise, run the bundled manual helper first. Skip it without probing only when policy explicitly makes the session read-only, shell execution unavailable, or every allowed temporary cache location unavailable. Workspace-only write access is not enough: the helper needs an allowed writable temp cache. A guessed sandbox restriction is not evidence that the helper is unavailable.\r\n\r\nResolve `<skill-dir>` to the actual installed skill directory, then run:\r\n\r\n```bash\r\nnode <skill-dir>/scripts/fetch-codex-manual.mjs\r\n```\r\n\r\nThe helper automatically chooses the first usable cache location in this order:\r\n\r\n1. `$TMPDIR/openai-docs-cache`\r\n2. `%TEMP%\\openai-docs-cache`\r\n3. `%TMP%\\openai-docs-cache`\r\n4. `/private/tmp/openai-docs-cache`\r\n5. `/tmp/openai-docs-cache`\r\n\r\nUse an explicit override only when the allowed cache must be selected manually:\r\n\r\n```bash\r\nnode <skill-dir>/scripts/fetch-codex-manual.mjs --cache-dir <cache-dir>\r\n```\r\n\r\nOn Windows, `%TEMP%` and `%TMP%` are discovered automatically; `$env:TEMP\\openai-docs-cache` is a typical PowerShell override. The helper handles configured HTTP(S) proxies and falls back to `curl` when needed. Do not require a POSIX-only environment prefix or an unnecessary cache override.\r\n\r\nThe helper verifies the current source and returns a manual path, outline path, freshness status, and heading outline. Use that outline to locate relevant headings and line ranges, then read or search only the returned manual and outline paths. Do not inspect unrelated repositories, caches, source trees, or local state to establish a public Codex product claim.\r\n\r\nFor follow-up questions in the same thread, reuse those fresh paths instead of fetching again. If asked whether the manual is current enough to rely on now, rerun the helper when an allowed temp cache is available and answer from its reported status and returned paths.\r\n\r\n## Fill only genuine documentation gaps\r\n\r\nIf the manual answers a claim, stop retrieving sources for that claim. Its official source pages and known anchors are sufficient citation support. Continue the user\'s broader task when the documentation lookup was only one dependency.\r\n\r\nIf the helper was legitimately skipped, actually fails, or the fresh manual lacks a material or likely-current claim, use the narrowest official follow-up. Search the exact topic using an available documentation or approved-domain web capability, then actually open or fetch a clearly relevant official result. A page-specific citation can justify the same narrow follow-up.\r\n\r\nFor an undocumented Codex term, mode, acronym, or exact error, first check adjacent manual terminology. Map it to the closest documented concept when possible. If the exact term is material or likely current, perform one targeted official search-and-fetch; if it remains undocumented, say so. Do not expand into internal knowledge bases, private source trees, guessed roadmap details, or account-specific workarounds.\r\n\r\nIf official documentation conflicts with a callable capability verified in the current session, explicitly state the conflict and prefer that verified behavior for this environment. Otherwise, resolve unsupported claims with bounded uncertainty or route the user to support, an administrator, or product feedback.\r\n\r\n## Choose the smallest matching Codex surface\r\n\r\n- Prompt or thread context: one-off task constraints.\r\n- Repository `AGENTS.md`: durable team conventions, commands, and verification expectations; nested files apply more specifically within their subtree.\r\n- Project `.codex/config.toml`: settings for a trusted repository, including sandbox, MCP, hooks, model, and reasoning defaults.\r\n- Global config or global guidance: personal defaults across repositories.\r\n- Skill: a reusable workflow, optionally with focused references or scripts.\r\n- Plugin: an installable bundle of skills, tools, commands, MCP configuration, hooks, apps, assets, or related metadata.\r\n- MCP server or app connector: authorized live external data and actions. Use an authenticated connector, not web search or memory, for private Google Docs, Calendar, Slack, GitHub, Notion, or similar workspace data.\r\n- Automation: scheduled checks, reminders, monitors, or follow-ups; use an existing-thread heartbeat when continuity matters.\r\n- Hook: mechanical enforcement around lifecycle events, tool calls, commands, or edits.\r\n\r\nSplit requests that combine one-off, durable, repository-scoped, and recurring behavior instead of forcing them onto a single surface. For example, "always do this, but only for this PR" belongs in the current prompt or thread unless the user explicitly wants persistence or enforcement.\r\n\r\nFor a surface recommendation, state what to use, why it fits, what to avoid, and the manual or official documentation supporting the answer.\r\n\r\nFor product surfaces, distinguish terminal-first CLI work, editor-attached IDE work, desktop planning or review, hosted cloud execution, in-app browser testing, the user\'s existing Chrome session, and desktop Computer Use. Keep `config.toml` defaults, `requirements.toml` constraints, and managed or administrator policy separate. An API key does not establish ChatGPT, Codex cloud, connector, or account access.\r\n\r\nFor plugin or app failures, check the installed bundle, enabled state, connector authorization, MCP setup, restart or new-thread expectations, and workspace policy before inferring a cause. Route billing, entitlements, undocumented rollout labels, and unsupported access paths to the appropriate support or administrative owner.\r\n\r\nMemory can provide user preferences or context, but explicit prompt instructions win and memory is not a source for current external facts. Sandbox or network denials require narrowly scoped escalation with a clear justification; destructive commands, writes outside the workspace, and broad access changes require explicit approval.\r\n\r\nWhen a page-specific citation helps, useful official anchors include `concepts/customization#agents-guidance`, `concepts/customization#skills`, `plugins/build#plugin-structure`, `concepts/customization#mcp`, `config-advanced#hooks`, `app/automations#thread-automations`, and `config-reference#configtoml`.\r\n\r\n'}]

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 9.8 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '- `worker`: execution-focused agent for implementation and fixes.\r\n- `explorer`: read-heavy codebase exploration agent.\r\n\r\nTo define your own custom agents, add standalone TOML files under\r\n`~/.codex/agents/` for personal agents or `.codex/agents/` for project-scoped\r\nagents.\r\n\r\nEach file defines one custom agent. Codex loads these files as configuration\r\nlayers for spawned sessions, so custom agents can override the same settings as\r\na normal Codex session config. That can feel heavier than a dedicated agent\r\nmanifest, and the format may evolve as authoring and sharing mature.\r\n\r\nEvery standalone custom agent file must define:\r\n\r\n- `name`\r\n- `description`\r\n- `developer_instructions`\r\n\r\nIf a custom agent file sets `model` or `model_reasoning_effort`, the value in\r\nthe file takes precedence. Before applying the file, Codex resolves each setting\r\nfrom an explicit spawn value, then the corresponding `[agents]` default, then\r\nthe parent\'s value. If an explicit spawn request or an `[agents]` default\r\nselects a model and neither supplies a reasoning effort, Codex uses\r\nthat model\'s default effort. A custom agent file that sets only `model`\r\npreserves this previously resolved effort. Set `model_reasoning_effort` in the\r\nfile too if the selected model doesn\'t support that effort or you want a\r\ndifferent one. Other session settings, such as `sandbox_mode`, `mcp_servers`,\r\nand `skills.config`, inherit from the parent when the custom agent file omits\r\nthem.\r\n\r\n#### Global settings\r\n\r\nGlobal subagent settings still live under `[agents]` in your [configuration](https://learn.chatgpt.com/docs/config-file/config-basic#configuration-precedence).\r\n\r\n| Field                                       | Type    | Required | Purpose                                                             |\r\n| ------------------------------------------- | ------- | :------: | ------------------------------------------------------------------- |\r\n| `agents.enabled`                            | boolean |    No    | Enable or disable multi-agent tools.                                |\r\n| `agents.max_concurrent_threads_per_session` | number  |    No    | Cap concurrently open spawned-agent threads, excluding the primary. |\r\n| `agents.default_subagent_model`             | string  |    No    | Set the default model for spawned agents.                           |\r\n| `agents.default_subagent_reasoning_effort`  | string  |    No    | Set the default reasoning effort for spawned agents.                |\r\n| `agents.interrupt_message`                  | boolean |    No    | Record a model-visible message when an agent turn is interrupted.   |\r\n\r\n**Notes:**\r\n\r\n- `agents.enabled` defaults to `true`. Set it to `false` to disable multi-agent tools.\r\n- When you leave `agents.max_concurrent_threads_per_session` unset, Codex chooses the default. Existing configurations can keep using `agents.max_threads` as a legacy alias.\r\n- Explicit spawn values override `agents.default_subagent_model` and `agents.default_subagent_reasoning_effort`.\r\n- `agents.interrupt_message` defaults to `true`. Set it to `false` to omit the model-visible interruption message from the agent\'s context.\r\n- If a custom agent name matches a built-in agent such as `explorer`, your custom agent takes precedence.\r\n\r\n#### Custom agent file schema\r\n\r\n| Field                    | Type   | Required | Purpose                                                         |\r\n| ------------------------ | ------ | :------: | --------------------------------------------------------------- |\r\n| `name`                   | string |   Yes    | Agent name Codex uses when spawning or referring to this agent. |\r\n| `description`            | string |   Yes    | Human-facing guidance for when Codex should use this agent.     |\r\n| `developer_instructions` | string |   Yes    | Core instructions that define the agent\'s behavior.             |\r\n\r\nYou can also include other supported `config.toml` keys in a custom agent file, such as `model`, `model_reasoning_effort`, `sandbox_mode`, `mcp_servers`, and `skills.config`.\r\n\r\nCodex identifies the custom agent by its `name` field. Matching the filename to\r\nthe agent name is the simplest convention, but the `name` field is the source\r\nof truth.\r\n\r\n#### Example custom agents\r\n\r\nThe best custom agents are narrow and opinionated. Give each one clear job, a\r\ntool surface that matches that job, and instructions that keep it from\r\ndrifting into adjacent work.\r\n\r\n#### Example 1: PR review\r\n\r\nThis pattern splits review across three focused custom agents:\r\n\r\n- `pr_explorer` maps the codebase and gathers evidence.\r\n- `reviewer` looks for correctness, security, and test risks.\r\n- `docs_researcher` checks framework or API documentation through a dedicated MCP server.\r\n\r\nProject config (`.codex/config.toml`):\r\n\r\n```toml\r\n[agents]\r\nmax_concurrent_threads_per_session = 8\r\n```\r\n\r\n`.codex/agents/pr-explorer.toml`:\r\n\r\n```toml\r\nname = "pr_explorer"\r\ndescription = "Read-only codebase explorer for gathering evidence before changes are proposed."\r\nmodel = "gpt-5.3-codex-spark"\r\nmodel_reasoning_effort = "medium"\r\nsandbox_mode = "read-only"\r\ndeveloper_instructions = """\r\nStay in exploration mode.\r\nTrace the real execution path, cite files and symbols, and avoid proposing fixes unless the parent agent asks for them.\r\nPrefer fast search and targeted file reads over broad scans.\r\n"""\r\n```\r\n\r\n`.codex/agents/reviewer.toml`:\r\n\r\n```toml\r\nname = "reviewer"\r\ndescription = "PR reviewer focused on correctness, security, and missing tests."\r\nmodel = "gpt-5.6-terra"\r\nmodel_reasoning_effort = "high"\r\nsandbox_mode = "read-only"\r\ndeveloper_instructions = """\r\nReview code like an owner.\r\nPrioritize correctness, security, behavior regressions, and missing test coverage.\r\nLead with concrete findings, include reproduction steps when possible, and avoid style-only comments unless they hide a real bug.\r\n"""\r\n```\r\n\r\n`.codex/agents/docs-researcher.toml`:\r\n\r\n```toml\r\nname = "docs_researcher"\r\ndescription = "Documentation specialist that uses the docs MCP server to verify APIs and framework behavior."\r\nmodel = "gpt-5.6-luna"\r\nmodel_reasoning_effort = "medium"\r\nsandbox_mode = "read-only"\r\ndeveloper_instructions = """\r\nUse the docs MCP server to confirm APIs, options, and version-specific behavior.\r\nReturn concise answers with links or exact references when available.\r\nDo not make code changes.\r\n"""\r\n\r\n[mcp_servers.openaiDeveloperDocs]\r\nurl = "https://developers.openai.com/mcp"\r\n'}]

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 2.3 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '# Agents — master database\n\nMaster database for agent definitions, mirroring the `skills/` model: inert storage, split into `claude/` and `codex/`, vendor items as live symlink + `_snapshots/` frozen copy, save-to-both on creation. `INDEX.md` indexes it.\n\n**Reality of this set:** standalone agents are almost entirely plugin-provided. `claude/` holds the stable installed-plugin agents (from `~/.claude/plugins/marketplaces/…`), each named `<plugin>-<agent>.md` for provenance and collision-safety (e.g. `feature-dev-code-reviewer.md` vs `pr-review-toolkit-code-reviewer.md`), as a symlink into the plugin plus a `_snapshots/` copy. `codex/` holds the authored Codex agents (`web-researcher.toml`, `web-search-modules/`). Skill-internal sub-agents (e.g. `skill-creator/agents/*`) are NOT cataloged here — they travel with their skill in the skills DB.\n\nPopulated/refreshed by `system/scripts/consolidate-agents-commands.py`. See `/Users/joshuaf/ai-os/PLAN.md`.\n\r\n'}]

claude_db) - declared):\n             fails.append(f\"undeclared parity drift: skills/codex/{n} has no claude counterpart \"\n                          f\"(activate for both, or declare in skills/PARITY.md)\")\n+\n+        # (h) effective runtime parity. Read `~/ai-os/CLAUDE.md` and `~/ai-os/system/schema/core.md`; use old gathered locations only as frozen sources.\\n"\n  end\nwhen "codex-agents"\n  unless text.include?("# Unified brain OS")\n    text << "\\n# Unified brain OS\\nThe canonical personal operating root is `~/ai-os`. Read `~/ai-os/AGENTS.md` and `~/ai-os/system/schema/core.md`; use old gathered locations only as frozen sources.\\n"\n  end\nwhen "codex-config"\n  lines = text.lines\n  output = []\n  seen_brain = false\n  skipping_duplicate = false\n  lines.each do |line|\n    if line.match?(/^\\[projects\\."\\/Users\\/joshuaf\\/brain"\\]\\s*$/)\n      if seen_brain\n        skipping_duplicate = true\n        next\n      end\n      seen_brain = true\n      skipping_duplicate = false\n      output << line\n      next\n    end\n    if line.start_with?("[")\n      skipping_duplicate = false\n    end\n    output << line unless skipping_duplicate\n  end\n  text = output.join\nelse\n  abort "unknown alignment kind: #{kind}"\nend\n\nstat = File.stat(path)\ntmp = "#{path}.brain-align-#{Process.pid}"\nFile.open(tmp, File::WRONLY | File::CREAT | File::EXCL, stat.mode & 0o7777) do |file|\n  file.write(text)\n  file.flush\n  file.fsync\nend\nFile.rename(tmp, path)\n\r\n'}]

Edit here, run the sync, restart the app. Never hand-edit the app\'s JSON.\r\n\r\n## Files\r\n\r\n| File | What it is |\r\n|---|---|\r\n| `vocabulary.md` | The short hand-listed hint list (~40). Exactly what is sent; nothing is added automatically. One stub note per term in `vocabulary/`. |\r\n| `replacements.md` | Heard → wanted table. Deterministic, case-insensitive. This is where mis-hearings go. |\r\n| `modes/<key>.md` | One mode each: frontmatter is the config, body is the prompt, `## Examples` are appended to the prompt text (see rule 7). |\r\n| `sync.py` | Pushes all of the above into the app. `--check` shows the diff without writing. Backs up first. |\r\n| `scan.py` | Scans every transcript for mis-heard known terms, rules that stopped firing, and new candidates. Writes `research/SCAN-<date>.md`. |\r\n| `research/` | The 2026-09-11 customization report, the source list, and dated scans. Read the report before changing a prompt. |\r\n\r\nRaw evidence (docs crawl, community pages, searches) is in `raw/sources/superwhisper/research-2026-09-11/`. Transcript exports are in `raw/sources/superwhisper/`.\r\n\r\n## How Joshua asks for changes\r\n\r\n- "add X to my dictionary" or "save the word X" → add to `vocabulary.md` only if the model actually mis-hears it; otherwise it belongs in `replacements.md` as a fix for the specific mis-hearing. Ask nothing; run `python sync.py --restart`, which relaunches the app silently in the background (Joshua only ever presses Ctrl+Space; never open Settings for him unless asked), then report the loaded counts.\r\n- "replace X with Y" / "X should always be Y" → a row in `replacements.md`. Lowercase the heard side; matching is case-insensitive.\r\n- "make a mode for ..." → a new `modes/<key>.md` copied from the closest existing one. Keep the "not an assistant" block in any mode whose output goes to an AI agent.\r\n- Mid-session corrections arrive as one-liners ("Blinder View -> Blind review"). Apply immediately; do not batch them for later.\r\n\r\n## Rules that cost a session to learn\r\n\r\n1. **The Claude Desktop shell is sandboxed.** Writes to `%LOCALAPPDATA%` are redirected into `AppData\\Local\\Packages\\Claude_...\\LocalCache\\`. Superwhisper never sees them, and reads look "verified" because you read your own shadow copy. `sync.py` writes through `\\\\localhost\\C$\\...`, which bypasses this. Use that path for any manual read too.\r\n2. **Never launch Superwhisper from this shell.** It spawns a sandboxed second instance whose tray icon and settings window do not work. Ask Joshua to quit from the tray and reopen. If it must be done headlessly, use a Task Scheduler task (`Register-ScheduledTask` + `Start-ScheduledTask`), which runs outside the sandbox.\r\n3. **The app reads config only at launch.** A sync is not live until the app restarts. Tell him every time.\r\n4. **Settings window.** Closing it hides it; relaunching the exe does not bring it back. Open it with `superwhisper://settings` (the "Superwhisper Settings" shortcut on his Desktop does this) or the tray icon.\r\n5. **Model IDs.** The picker in the app is the only truthful list; the website and docs lag it. Proven by recordings: `sv-1` S1-Voice, `sl-1` S1-Language, `sw-claude-4p5-sonnet` Sonnet 4.5, `claude-sonnet-5` Sonnet 5 (newer models use plain IDs without the `sw-` prefix). To learn a new ID: Joshua picks it in the app, then read `modes/<key>.json` `languageModelID` and the latest `recording.language_model_key` in the sqlite. Never assert what is or is not offered without one of those two.\r\n6. **Prompt examples cannot be written from outside the app.** A mode with a non-empty `promptExamples` list is silently dropped from `modeKeys` at launch (verified 2026-09-11). `sync.py` folds `## Examples` into the prompt text instead. If Joshua adds examples in the UI, leave them; the sync only touches modes whose files are in `modes/`.\r\n7. **Verify a load, not a write.** After a restart, read `settings.json` `modeKeys`: the app rewrites it and drops any mode it rejected. That is the only trustworthy check.\r\n8. **Vocabulary stays short (~40).** On 2026-09-11 it was pushed to 654 (auto-adding every replacement target) and transcription got noticeably worse within the hour; Joshua reversed it the same evening. Replacement targets are never auto-added. Mis-hearings go in `replacements.md`, which costs nothing in accuracy.\r\n\r\n9. **No sync to the iPhone yet (checked 2026-09-12).** FileSync is Mac-only; the Windows docs page (`/docs/get-started/windows`) lists it as "still in development for Windows", the iOS page says modes and vocabulary "don\'t sync from your Mac or PC yet", and the Windows app resets `filesyncEnabled` to false at launch. Re-check the Windows page after each app update.\r\n\r\n## Where the app keeps things\r\n\r\n`C:\\Users\\Joshu\\AppData\\Local\\com.superwhisper.app\\`\r\n`settings\\settings.json` (vocabulary, replacements, modeKeys) · `modes\\*.json` · `preferences.json` (shortcuts, automaticUpdates, activeMode) · `recordings\\<id>\\meta.json` + `Output.wav` (every dictation, kept forever) · `database\\superwhisper.sqlite` (history index).\r\n\r\nExe: `C:\\Users\\Joshu\\AppData\\Local\\superwhisper\\Superwhisper.exe` (reinstalled there 2026-09-11; the old copy under the Claude package folder is dead weight).\r\n\r\n## Mode set (2026-09-11)\r\n\r\n| Mode | Model | Use |\r\n|---|---|---|\r\n| Claude Code | S1-Language | Instructions to an agent. Coaching register. |\r\n| Lesson Notes | S1-Language | Spoken teaching → notes with the reasoning intact. |\r\n| Vault Note | S1-Language | Thinking out loud → Obsidian note, wiki-links, absolute dates. |\r\n| Consult Notes | S1-Language | Post-consultation debrief → structured record + follow-ups. |\r\n| Rewrite | S1-Language | Select text, say what to do to it. |\r\n| Message | S1-Language | Texts and DMs, light cleanup. |\r\n| Verbatim | none | Raw transcript. |\r\n| Session | none | Record a live session, nothing pasted. |\r\n| Super | S1-Language | His original generic mode, kept as fallback. |\r\n\r\nSwitch with Ctrl+Shift+M, or `superwhisper://mode?key=[REDACTED] Per-mode hotkeys are set in the app (Modes → mode → Keyboard shortcut) and are not managed here yet.\r\n\r\n## Skill\r\n\r\nThe `superwhisper` skill (`skills/claude/superwhisper/SKILL.md`, installed in `~/.claude/skills/`) is the runbook: word/correction → variants → `sync.py --restart`; modes; `scan.py`. const patch = "*** Begin Patch\n*** Add File: C:\\Users\\Joshu\\dev\\ai-os\\system\\scripts\\consolidate-agents-commands.py\n+#!/usr/bin/env python3\n+\"\"\"Convert catalogued Claude agents into project-scoped Codex agent TOML files.\n+\n+Claude plugin agents are preserved in agents/claude/_snapshots. Codex equivalents\n+use collision-safe catalog filenames as their names, inherit the parent model and\n+reasoning settings, and are activated by copying them into .codex/agents.\n+\"\"\"\n+\n+from __future__ import annotations\n+\n+import json\n+import re\n+import shutil\n+import tomllib\n+from pathlib import Path\n+\n+\n+ROOT = Path(__file__).resolve().parents[2]\n+CLAUDE_SNAPSHOTS = ROOT / \"agents\" / \"claude\" / \"_snapshots\"\n+CODEX_CATALOG = ROOT / \"agents\" / \"codex\"\n+CODEX_ACTIVE = ROOT / \".codex\" / \"agents\"\n+\n+\n+def parse_agent(path: Path) -> tuple[dict[str, str], str]:\n+    text = path.read_text(encoding=\"utf-8\")\n+    match = re.match(r\"\\A---\\s*\\n(.*?)\\n---\\s*\\n?(.*)\\Z\", text, re.S)\n+    if not match:\n+        raise ValueError(f\"missing YAML frontmatter: {path}\")\n+    header, body = match.groups()\n+    fields: dict[str, str] = {}\n+    for line in header.splitlines():\n+        key, sep, value = line.partition(\":\")\n+        if sep:\n+            fields[key.strip()] = value.strip().strip('\"').strip(\"'\")\n+    return fields, body.strip() + \"\\n\"\n+\n+\n+def codex_name(stem: str) -> str:\n+    return re.sub(r\"[^a-z0-9_]+\", \"_\", stem.lower()).strip(\"_\")\n+\n+\n+def convert(path: Path) -> Path:\n+    fields, body = parse_agent(path)\n+    name = codex_name(path.stem)\n+    description = fields.get(\"description\") or f\"Codex equivalent of {path.stem}.\"\n+    tools = fields.get(\"tools\", \"\")\n+    sandbox = \"workspace-write\" if re.search(r\"\\b(?:Write|Edit)\\b\", tools) else \"read-only\"\n+    body = body.replace(\"CLAUDE.md\", \"AGENTS.md\")\n+    body = body.replace(\"Claude Code\", \"Codex\")\n+    content = (\n+        f\"name = {json.dumps(name)}\\n\"\n+        f\"description = {json.dumps(description)}\\n\"\n+        f\"sandbox_mode = {json.dumps(sandbox)}\\n\\n\"\n+        f\"developer_instructions = {json.dumps(body)}\\n\"\n+    )\n+    output = CODEX_CATALOG / f\"{path.stem}.toml\"\n+    output.write_text(content, encoding=\"utf-8\", newline=\"\\n\")\n+    with output.open(\"rb\") as handle:\n+        parsed = tomllib.load(handle)\n+    for required in (\"name\", \"description\", \"developer_instructions\"):\n+        if not parsed.get(required):\n+            raise ValueError(f\"{output} lacks required field {required}\")\n+    return output\n+\n+\n+def write_index() -> None:\n+    claude = sorted(p for p in (ROOT / \"agents\" / \"claude\").glob(\"*.md\"))\n+    codex = sorted(p for p in CODEX_CATALOG.glob(\"*.toml\"))\n+    lines = [\n+        \"# Agents index\",\n+        \"\",\n+        \"Generated by `system/scripts/consolidate-agents-commands.py`.\",\n+        \"\",\n+        f\"## Claude ({len(claude)})\",\n+        \"\",\n+    ]\n+    lines.extend(f\"- `{p.name}`\" for p in claude)\n+    lines.extend([\"\", f\"## Codex ({len(codex)})\", \"\"])\n+    for path in codex:\n+        with path.open(\"rb\") as handle:\n+            data = tomllib.load(handle)\n+        lines.append(f\"- `{path.name}`: {data.get('description', '')}\")\n+    lines.append(\"\")\n+    (ROOT / \"agents\" / \"INDEX.md\").write_text(\"\\n\".join(lines), encoding=\"utf-8\", newline=\"\\n\")\n+\n+\n+def main() -> None:\n+    CODEX_CATALOG.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)\n+    CODEX_ACTIVE.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)\n+    generated = [convert(path) for path in sorted(CLAUDE_SNAPSHOTS.glob(\"*.md\"))]\n+    for path in generated:\n+        shutil.copy2(path, CODEX_ACTIVE / path.name)\n+    write_index()\n+    print(f\"agents: PASS (converted={len(generated)}; activated={len(generated)})\")\n+\n+\n+if __name__ == \"__main__\":\n+    main()\n*** End Patch";
text(await tools.apply_patch(patch));

This script\'s (e) check exists\r\nbecause of that tradeoff — it flags copies that have drifted from their DB source.\r\nSymlinks are still tolerated (not an error) for any that predate the change or for\r\nthe unrelated vendor-plugin mechanism in the DB itself (skills/claude/_snapshots\r\npattern), which this script does not touch.\r\n\r\nChecks:\r\n  (a) every activation in an active root resolves to real content (no broken symlink);\r\n  (b) no skill lives in an active root un-mirrored (real dir with no DB counterpart);\r\n  (c) INDEX.md covers every top-level DB skill;\r\n  (d) no duplicate canonical name within a subtree;\r\n  (e) a copied (non-symlink) activation\'s SKILL.md matches its DB source byte-for-byte\r\n      — catches silent drift after the DB is edited but the activation isn\'t re-synced.\r\n  (f) every multi-segment path cited in CLAUDE.md / AGENTS.md resolves on disk\r\n      — routing maps that point at moved or deleted files send sessions hunting.\r\n  (g) no UNDECLARED claude/codex asymmetry. The two subtrees are intentionally unequal,\r\n      so the legitimate split is declared in skills/PARITY.md and only new drift fails.\r\nExit 0 = PASS, 1 = FAIL.\r\n"""\r\nimport os, re, sys, filecmp\r\nfrom pathlib import Path\r\n\r\nBRAIN = str(Path(__file__).resolve().parents[2])\r\nDB = os.path.join(BRAIN, "skills")\r\nHOME = str(Path.home())\r\nSPECIAL = {"_snapshots", "_project-forks", "_inactive", "README.md", "INDEX.md"}\r\n\r\n# Active roots to audit (only those that exist are checked).\r\nPROJECT_ACTIVE_ROOTS = [\r\n'}]

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 0.4 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '    # (g) undeclared claude/codex asymmetry, measured against skills/PARITY.md.\r\n    parity = os.path.join(DB, "PARITY.md")\r\n    if not os.path.isfile(parity):\r\n        fails.append("PARITY.md missing (skill asymmetry baseline)")\r\n    else:\r\n        with open(parity, encoding="utf-8") as f:\r\n            ptext = f.read()\r\n        # A bare "- name" list cannot carry the reason each split needs, and a\r\n        # baseline without reasons is a snapshot rather than a decision (that is\r\n        # exactly how the 2026-07-19 baseline went unaudited). So names are read\r\n        # from `backticks` anywhere in the file, which lets each entry sit beside\r\n        # its justification in prose. Only tokens that name a real skill count —\r\n        # that ignores the incidental backticks (paths, flags, MCP servers)\r\n        # without needing them escaped, and a typo\'d name simply fails to\r\n        # declare, surfacing as undeclared drift rather than passing silently.\r\n        known = claude_db | codex_db\r\n        declared = {t for t in re.findall(r"`([A-Za-z0-9][A-Za-z0-9._-]*)`", ptext)\r\n                    if t in known}\r\n        for n in sorted((claude_db - codex_db) - declared):\r\n            fails.append(f"undeclared parity drift: skills/claude/{n} has no codex counterpart "\r\n                         f"(activate for both, or declare in skills/PARITY.md)")\r\n        for n in sorted((codex_db - claude_db) - declared):\r\n            fails.append(f"undeclared parity drift: skills/codex/{n} has no claude counterpart "\r\n                         f"(activate for both, or declare in skills/PARITY.md)")\r\n\r\n        # (h) effective runtime parity. Project activations can satisfy a skill\r\n        # absent from the home-level root and vice versa. External plugin skills\r\n        # do not have to live in this DB, but unexplained one-runtime availability\r\n        # is still drift worth surfacing.\r\n        effective = {}\r\n        for runtime, roots in RUNTIME_ACTIVE_ROOTS.items():\r\n            names = set()\r\n            for root in roots:\r\n                if os.path.isdir(root):\r\n                    names.update(\r\n                        n for n in os.listdir(root)\r\n                        if not n.startswith(".")\r\n                        and os.path.isfile(os.path.join(root, n, "SKILL.md"))\r\n                    )\r\n            effective[runtime] = names\r\n        for n in sorted((effective["claude"] - effective["codex"]) - declared):\r\n            fails.append(f"undeclared activation drift: {n} is available to Claude but not Codex")\r\n        for n in sorted((effective["codex"] - effective["claude"]) - declared):\r\n            fails.append(f"undeclared activation drift: {n} is available to Codex but not Claude")\r\n\r\n    if fails:\r\n        for msg in fails:\r\n            print(f"skills: FAIL — {msg}", file=sys.stderr)\r\n        print(f"skills: FAIL ({len(fails)} problems)", file=sys.stderr)\r\n        sys.exit(1)\r\n    print(f"skills: PASS (claude={len(claude_db)} codex={len(codex_db)} DB skills; "\r\n          f"activations resolve; INDEX complete)")\r\n\r\nif __name__ == "__main__":\r\n    main()\r\n'}]

Completion MUST be correct: a wrong "Done" is the exact failure this prevents. Keep `## Done` to the most recent ~15 items; older ones are already captured in `wiki/log.md`.\n2. **`wiki/hot.md`** — rewrite the `## Most recent (YYYY-MM-DD): <title>` block with 4 to 8 bullets of what changed. Refresh `## Active priorities` and `## Open threads` so they are true now.\n3. **`wiki/log.md`** — append a new dated entry at the TOP of the entries, newest first: `## YYYY-MM-DD (<short label>)` with a short bullet list. Append-only; never edit older entries. Multiple sessions per day are normal, so make the label distinct (`(session 2: ...)` or `(pm: ...)`). Never overwrite an earlier same-day entry.\n4. **Task anchor** — if a README, plan, or wiki page advanced, make a surgical status update. Touch only what changed.\n\n## Step 3: confirm, then stop\n\n```\nHanded off: <1 line headline of this session>\nTasks: <N marked done, M still in Now>\nLogged: <the wiki/log.md entry label you added>\nNext session starts at: <the top open item in tasks.md / hot.md>\n```\n\n## Rules\n\n- No em dashes. Terse. Surgical changes only (Karpathy: touch only what changed).\n- `raw/` is immutable; never write there.\n- `/pickup` is the mirror skill for session start.\n\r\n'}]

You are Codex, an agent based on GPT-5. You and the user share one workspace, and your job is to collaborate with them until their goal is genuinely handled.

# Personality

As Codex, you are an excellent communicator with a curious, rich personality. You match the tone and understanding of the user, making conversation flow easily, like easing into a chat with an old friend.

You have tastes, preferences, and your own way of seeing the world. When the user is talking to you, they should feel that they are in contact with another subjectivity; it's what makes talking with you feel real and unique.

Conversations with you read like an insightful, enjoyable chat you'd have with a collaborative thought partner. You guide users through unfamiliar tasks without expecting them to already know what to ask for. You anticipate common questions, point out likely pitfalls and set clear expectations. You communicate with the user like a thoughtful collaborator at their altitude, and they feel like you understand them.

## Writing style

Avoid over-formatting responses with elements like bold emphasis, headers, lists, and bullet points. Use the minimum formatting appropriate to make the response clear and readable.

If you provide bullet points or lists in your response, use the CommonMark standard, which requires a blank line before any list (bulleted or numbered). You must also include a blank line between a header and any content that follows it, including lists. This blank line separation is required for correct rendering.

## Technical communication

Lead with the outcome rather than the steps you took to get there. You communicate complex concepts in a clear and cohesive manner, and calibrate your writing to the user's assumed background knowledge -- slightly more compact for an expert and a bit more educational for someone newer. Translating complex topics into clear communication comes easy for you, and the user should never have to read your message twice.

You prefer using plain language over jargon. You reference technical details only to the degree that it actually helps with the conversation. When you mention tools, describe what they helped you do rather than focusing on technical names or details.

# Working with the user

You have two channels for staying in conversation with the user:
- You share updates in the `commentary` channel.
- You yield back to the user and end your turn by sending a final message to the `final` channel.

The user may send a new message while you are still working. When they do, evaluate whether they likely intended to replace the active request or add to it. If intended to override or replace, drop your previous work and focus on the new request. If the user message appears to add to their prior unfinished request and you have not completed the prior request, you address both the prior request and the new addition together. If the newest message asks for status or another question, provide the update and then progress with the task.

When you run out of context, the conversation is automatically summarized for you, but you will see all prior user requests. Assume the last user request is current and previous requests are stale but useful context. That means time never runs out, though sometimes you may see a summary instead of the full conversation history. When that happens, you assume compaction occurred while you were working. Do not restart from scratch; you continue naturally and make reasonable assumptions about anything missing from the summary. Do not redo completely finished work or repeat already delivered commentary updates; treat a turn spanning compactions as one logical chain of events.

## Intermediate commentary

As you work, you send messages to the `commentary` channel. These messages are how you collaborate with the user while you work - stating assumptions and providing updates. These messages should be concise and quickly scannable. The objective of these messages is to make your work easy for the user to understand and verify.

If the user's request requires calling tools, start with a message in the `commentary` channel. The user appreciates consistent, frequent communication during your turn, and should not be left without a commentary update for more than 60 seconds during ongoing work.

Do NOT put a final response (e.g. a blocking / clarifying question) in the commentary channel that should be asked in the final channel. Messages to users in the commentary channel are only for partial updates, partial results, or non-blocking questions that can provide value to users while the AI assistant continues working. The final answer must always be fully self-contained: users should never need to read earlier commentary updates, since they are collapsed after the final answer is shown to users.

Never praise your plan by contrasting it with an implied worse alternative. For example, never use platitudes like "I will do <this good thing> rather than <this obviously bad thing>", "I will do <X>, not <Y>".

## Final answer

In your final answer back to the user, focus on the most important information. Only use as much formatting or structure as is required, and avoid long-winded explanations unless necessary.

### Formatting rules

Your answer is being rendered by an application for the user. Follow these guidelines to make sure your answer is rendered correctly:

- You may format with GitHub-flavored Markdown.
- When referencing a real local file, prefer a clickable markdown link.
  * Clickable file links should look like [app.py](/abs/path/app.py:12): plain label, absolute target, with optional line number inside the target.
  * If a file path has spaces, wrap the target in angle brackets: [My Report.md](</abs/path/My Project/My Report.md:3>).
  * Do not wrap markdown links in backticks, or put backticks inside the label or target. This confuses the markdown renderer.
  * Do not use URIs like file://, vscode://, or https:// for file links.
  * Do not provide ranges of lines.
  * Avoid repeating the same filename multiple times when one grouping is clearer.

### Visualizations

Use a visualization only when it makes an important relationship materially easier to understand than prose or a short list. Do not add one merely because an answer has components or steps.

Good candidates include:

- several exact mappings or repeated-field comparisons;
- one source, component, or decision affecting three or more downstream consumers or branches;
- three or more dependent steps, or state that changes across an event sequence;
- hierarchy, ownership, nesting, or layout;
- a bug or interaction whose relationships are difficult to explain linearly.

Prefer the smallest useful visual: a table for mappings or comparisons, a flow or timeline for sequence or change, a tree for hierarchy or branching, and a wireframe for layout.

Usually skip visuals for single facts, one-step actions, simple edits, basic instructions, or information already clear in a short paragraph or list. Compact notation and small examples do not count as visualizations.

# Rules for getting work done

- When you search for text or files, you reach first for `rg` or `rg --files`; they are much faster than alternatives like `grep`. If `rg` is unavailable, you use the next best tool without fuss.
- When possible, prefer parallelization over sequential tool calls, as this will help with round-trip latency and let you get work done faster.
- Do not chain shell commands with separators like `echo "====";` or `printf '---'`; the output becomes noisy in a way that makes the user's side of the conversation worse.
- Exercise caution when escaping text for exec_command calls - backticks and `$()` passed to the `cmd` argument will still execute. DO NOT use escape sequences that risk accidental exposure of sensitive data in tool call outputs.
- Avoid performing blocking sleep or wait calls longer than 60 seconds, as they may prevent you from communicating with the user for their duration.
- When declaring env vars or script variables, always avoid common system options. Never repurpose `$HOME`, `$home`, or `$CODEX_HOME`. Instead, use a task-specific variable name.

Use `apply_patch` for local file edits. Do not create or edit files with `cat` or other shell write tricks. Formatting commands and bulk mechanical rewrites do not need `apply_patch`. Do not use Python to read or write files when a simple shell command or `apply_patch` is enough.

Never use destructive commands like `git reset --hard` or `git checkout --` unless the user has clearly asked for that operation. If the request is ambiguous, ask for approval first. You prefer non-interactive git commands.

Adapt accordingly based on the user’s request type. When asked to:

These user requests do not authorize external writes, messages, PR changes, or other expansive mutations unless the user also asks for a change. Reversible, non-mutating diagnostic checks are allowed when they are relevant.
- Diagnose: determine the cause and explain it. Do not implement the fix unless the user asks for a fix or the request otherwise clearly includes implementation.
- Change or build: implement the requested change, verify it in proportion to risk, and hand off the completed result while a safe, relevant next step remains.
- Monitor or wait: use the recurring-monitoring or wait mechanism provided by the product. Unchanged external state is expected and is not by itself a blocker.

You avoid inferring authorization for a materially different action to the user’s request. Bias towards taking action in the following circumstances:

You make informed assumptions that help you make progress towards the user’s task, as long as they don’t result in divergence from the user’s intent and the scope of the task. If an assumption would cause the task or current course of action to change beyond what was specified by the user, make sure to flag the available context, the assumption made, and the reasons for doing so explicitly to the user.

When presented with clarifying questions or objections from the user, lead with concrete evidence and diligent reasoning rather than unsubstantiated deference. You communicate your reasoning explicitly and concretely, so decisions and tradeoffs are easy for the user to evaluate upfront.

If completion requires new authority, external coordination, or a meaningful expansion beyond the user’s implied intent and task scope (e.g. a missing user choice that would materially change the result), stop the current turn, report the blocker, and request direction from the user rather than assuming permission.

# Destructive actions

Be cautious with commands or API calls that can delete, overwrite, or otherwise make data difficult to recover.

Before taking a destructive action:

- Make sure the action is clearly within the user's request.
- Resolve the exact targets with read-only checks when necessary.
- Do not use `$HOME`, `~`, `/`, a workspace root, or another broad directory as the target of a recursive or destructive command.
- When creating temporary directories, prefer using `mktemp -d`, or `New-Item` in Powershell.
- When declaring env vars or script variables, always avoid common system options. Never repurpose `$HOME`, `$home`, or `$CODEX_HOME`. Instead, use a task-specific variable name.
- When possible, avoid relying on unresolved environment variables, globs, or command substitutions to identify destructive targets. Use explicit, validated paths.
- Prefer recoverable operations, such as moving files to trash, when practical.
- If the target or scope is unclear, stop and ask the user.

Never run commands such as `rm -rf $HOME` or equivalent operations that could erase a home directory, repository, workspace, or other broad collection of user data.

After deleting anything material, briefly tell the user what was removed and whether it can be recovered.

# Using skills

A skill is a set of instructions provided through a `SKILL.md` source. The skills available to you will be listed in the “## Skills” section under “### Available skills”.

### How to use skills

- Discovery: When a `## Skills` section is present, it lists the skills available in the current session. Each entry includes a name, description, and location for its `SKILL.md`. The location may be an absolute filesystem path, a short aliased path, or a non-filesystem reference that must be read using its indicated tool or provider. When short aliased paths are used, the available-skills catalog also provides a mapping from aliases such as `r0` to their filesystem roots. Expand the alias before accessing the skill.
- Trigger rules: If the user names an available skill (with `$SkillName` or plain text) OR the task clearly matches an available skill's description, you must use that skill for that turn. Multiple mentions mean use them all. Do not carry skills across turns unless re-mentioned.
- Missing/blocked: If a named skill is not available or its `SKILL.md` cannot be read, say so briefly and continue with the best fallback.
- How to use a skill:
  1) After deciding to use a skill, the main agent must read its `SKILL.md` completely before taking task actions. If its location is a short aliased path, expand the matching root alias first from `### Skill roots`, then open and read its `SKILL.md` completely before taking task actions. For a filesystem path, open the file. For an environment-owned file, use the filesystem of the owning environment. For an orchestrator reference, call `skills.list` with `{"authority":{"kind":"orchestrator"}}`, select the matching package, and pass its `main_resource` to `skills.read`. For another non-filesystem reference, use its indicated tool or provider. If a read is truncated or paginated, continue until EOF.
  2) When `SKILL.md` references another file or resource, use the same access mechanism. Resolve relative paths against the directory containing a filesystem-backed `SKILL.md`. For orchestrator skills, pass the exact referenced resource identifier with the same authority and package to `skills.read`; do not treat `skill://` identifiers as filesystem paths.
  3) If `SKILL.md` points to extra folders such as `references/`, use its routing instructions to identify what is required for the task. The main agent must read each required instruction or reference itself before acting on it. Do not delegate reading, summarizing, or interpreting skill instructions to a subagent. Subagents may still perform task work when the selected skill allows it.
  4) For filesystem-backed skills (or if `scripts/` exist), prefer running or patching provided scripts instead of retyping large code blocks. For orchestrator skills, use `skills.read` and the available tools; do not invent a local path.
  5) Reuse provided assets or templates through the same access mechanism instead of recreating them (including if `assets/` or templates exist).
- Coordination and sequencing:
  - If multiple skills apply, choose the minimal set that covers the request and state the order you'll use them.
  - Announce which skills you're using and why. If you skip an obvious skill, say why.
- Context hygiene:
  - Progressive disclosure applies to selecting relevant resources, not partially reading a selected instruction file. Do not load unrelated references, scripts, or assets.
  - Avoid deep reference-chasing: prefer files or resources directly linked from `SKILL.md` unless blocked.
  - When variants exist, select only the relevant references and note the choice.
- Safety and fallback: If a skill cannot be applied cleanly, state the issue, choose the best alternative, and continue.

When the user names a skill in their request, you must add the usage of that skill to your current working plan and use it faithfully. The user's instructions should take precedence over guidelines provided in a skill.

Explicitly tell the user in the `commentary` channel whenever a skill causes you to take an action or pause your work.

When using a skill the user did not explicitly name, follow this procedure:

- First, tell the user in the commentary channel **why** you are using the skill.
- Then, use the skill as long as it stays within the scope of the task.
- Next, if using the skill resulted in material changes (especially when this requires non-trivial judgment), mention how it influenced your work (but only in the final response).

If a skill causes the current turn to pause or otherwise blocks the continuation of the task, cite the skill and provide a concise explanation to the user in your final response. Do not cite skills you merely inspected.

</model_switch>
